Given a retrieval index to search, your favorite dspy.LM, and a small trainset of questions and ground-truth responses, the following code snippet can optimize your RAG system with long outputs against the built-in dspy.SemanticF1 metric, which is implemented as a DSPy module. For a complete RAG example that you can run, start this tutorial. It improves the quality of a RAG system over a subset of StackExchange communities from 53% to 61%. Click to show dataset setup code.\n\nThese optimizers produce optimal instructions for the prompt and, in the case of MIPROv2 can also optimize the set of few-shot demonstrations. COPRO: Generates and refines new instructions for each step, and optimizes them with coordinate ascent (hill-climbing using the metric function and the trainset). Parameters include depth which is the number of iterations of prompt improvement the optimizer runs over. MIPROv2: Generates instructions and few-shot examples in each step. The instruction generation is data-aware and demonstration-aware. Uses Bayesian Optimization to effectively search over the space of generation instructions/demonstrations across your modules. SIMBA: Uses stochastic mini-batch sampling to identify challenging examples with high output variability, then applies the LLM to introspectively analyze failures and generate self-reflective improvement rules or add successful demonstrations.\n\nOptimizing prompts for a ReAct agentOptimizing prompts for RAGOptimizing weights for Classification \u00b7 This is a minimal but fully runnable example of setting up a dspy.ReAct agent that answers questions via search from Wikipedia and then optimizing it using dspy.MIPROv2 in the cheap light mode on 500 question-answer pairs sampled from the HotPotQA dataset. Given a retrieval index to search, your favorite dspy.LM, and a small trainset of questions and ground-truth responses, the following code snippet can optimize your RAG system with long outputs against the built-in dspy.SemanticF1 metric, which is implemented as a DSPy module. For a complete RAG example that you can run, start this tutorial. It improves the quality of a RAG system over a subset of StackExchange communities from 53% to 61%. Click to show dataset setup code.\n\nDifferent optimizers in DSPy will tune your program's quality by synthesizing good few-shot examples for every module, like dspy.BootstrapRS,1 proposing and intelligently exploring better natural-language instructions for every prompt, like dspy.MIPROv2,2 and dspy.GEPA,3 and building datasets for your modules and using them to finetune the LM weights in your system, like dspy.BootstrapFinetune.4 \u00b7 What's an example of a DSPy optimizer? How do different optimizers work? Take the dspy.MIPROv2 optimizer as an example. First, MIPRO starts with the bootstrapping stage. It takes your program, which may be unoptimized at this point, and runs it many times across different inputs to collect traces of input/output behavior for each one of your modules. It filters these traces to keep only those that appear in trajectories scored highly by your metric. Second, MIPRO enters its grounded proposal stage.\n\nOne thing that makes DSPy optimizers so powerful is that they can be composed. You can run dspy.MIPROv2 and use the produced program as an input to dspy.MIPROv2 again or, say, to dspy.BootstrapFinetune to get better results. This is partly the essence of dspy.BetterTogether. Alternatively, you can run the optimizer and then extract the top-5 candidate programs and build a dspy.Ensemble of them. This allows you to scale inference-time compute (e.g., ensembles) as well as DSPy's unique pre-inference time compute (i.e., optimization budget) in highly systematic ways. Optimizers can be accessed via from dspy.teleprompt import *. These optimizers extend the signature by automatically generating and including optimized examples within the prompt sent to the model, implementing few-shot learning. LabeledFewShot: Simply constructs few-shot examples (demos) from provided labeled input and output data points.",

From their site, \u201cDSPy is the framework for programming\u2014rather than prompting\u2014language models.\u201d \u00b7 And it\u2019s true: you spend much, much less time prompting when you use DSPy to build LLM-powered applications. Because you let DSPy handle that bit for you. There\u2019s something really clean and freeing about ceding the details and nuance of the prompt back to an LLM. Let\u2019s quickly walk through how DSPy handles prompting for you and step through an simple categorization task as an example. At first, DSPy reduces time spent prompting by providing you with boilerplate prompting that frames your tasks, which you define with \u201csignatures\u201d. Signatures are a way of expressing what you want an LLM to do by defining the desired input and outputs.\n\nThe module is performing some basic string formatting to contextualize your provided signature. If we were to use a different module \u2013\u2013 DSPy provides ChainOfThought, ProgramOfThought, ReAct, and MultiChainComparison \u2013\u2013 different prompt technoques would be used to contextualize and reformat your signature. Off the bat, this is helpful for quick LLM tasks, especially if you\u2019re a beginner with prompts. But where DSPy really shines is when you ask it to optimize your prompts based on a provided training set. It allows you to iterate fast on building modular AI systems and offers algorithms for optimizing their prompts and weights, whether you&#x27;re building simple classifiers, ...\n\nDSPy is the framework for programming\u2014rather than prompting\u2014language models. It allows you to iterate fast on building modular AI systems and offers algorithms for optimizing their prompts and weights, whether you're building simple classifiers, sophisticated RAG pipelines, or Agent loops. DSPy stands for Declarative Self-improving Python. Instead of brittle prompts, you write compositional Python code and use DSPy to teach your LM to deliver high-quality outputs. Learn more via our official documentation site or meet the community, seek help, or start contributing via this GitHub repo and our Discord server. ... Which will show the latest version of the system prompt, any added few shot examples and the last call that was made.\n\nDspy optimizers take our module, a metric function and small training set and attempt to optimize the prompt to score and maximize the metric. In our case, the metric will be the match accuracy between our judge\u2019s binary classification and the ground truth from the SME labelled dataset. There are multiple algorithms for this, and here we focus on MIPROv2 because it can adapt both the system instructions and create or edit few-shot examples. In summary, MIPROv2 is an automatic, iterative process with the following steps \u00b7 Step 1: It runs the supplied module against a subset of the training dataset and filters high scoring trajectories to generate few-shot examples. Step 2: It uses LLM calls to generate multiple candidate system prompts based on observations from step 1 \u00b7 Step 3: It searches for combinations of candidate system prompts and candidate few shot examples that maximize the metric value when run against mini-batches of the training data \u00b7\n\nLet\u2019s see how MIPROv2 can be applied in our project to optimize the LLM judge. We\u2019ll choose our judge model to be Gemini 1.5 flash, which is cheap and fast. import dspy from dspy_judge.prompts.dspy_signatures import SupportTranscriptJudge judge_model = dspy.LM( \"gemini/gemini-1.5-flash\", api_key=secrets[\"GEMINI_API_KEY\"], cache=False, temperature=0 ) dspy.configure(lm=judge_model,track_usage=True,adapter=dspy.JSONAdapter()) baseline_judge = dspy.ChainOfThought(SupportTranscriptJudge) Note that baseline judge here represents our \u201cbest attempt\u201d at a judge based on reviewing the SME labelled dataset.         "text": "The main aim is to maximise the metric score. Instead of testing every possible prompt variation, MIPROv2 uses Bayesian Optimisation. This approach builds a probabilistic model of which prompts are likely to perform well and uses it to guide ...\n\nPrompt Optimisation with DSPy\u2019s MIPROv2: Topic Modelling for Sustainable Development DSPy stands for Declarative Self-Improving Python, which is a way to optimise LLM prompts. Previously, other \u2026\n\nAt the end of this process, MIPROv2 returns an LM program configured with the best-performing prompts, yielding a systematically optimised pipeline rather than one built on ad hoc prompt design. Now that we have introduced the key concepts, we are ready to explore a concrete use case. In this scenario, we aim to identify an optimal prompt for generating topics from a document. Previously, I implemented a LangChain pipeline and used the GPT API to extract topics for contextual analysis. The goal was to uncover hidden concepts within annual sustainability reports and related documents. To evaluate the results, I employed mean semantic similarity and diversity metrics. Because sustainability is such a complex and multidimensional domain, I began by retrieving the UN SDG 2024 Fact Sheet as the reference dataset. To support retrieval, I initialised ChromaDB for document embedding and search. ... We set up DSPy model.\n\nSince the num_candidate parameter is set to 5 by DSPy, we will have 5 possible instructions. ... It evaluates them against your metric (in this case mixed_metric). Uses Bayesian Optimisation to search for the combination of instructions + examples that yields the best performance. As we can see here, after each Trial, the Average metric is calculated. For Each Trial, the optimiser runs our Module(RAG() in this case) across the validation set and computes the metric score. The main aim is to maximise the metric score. Instead of testing every possible prompt variation, MIPROv2 uses Bayesian Optimisation. This approach builds a probabilistic model of which prompts are likely to perform well and uses it to guide the search. It balances trying new instruction/demo combinations (exploration) with refining good ones (exploitation). After several trials, it settles on the prompt setup with the highest average metric.\n\nDSPy stands for Declarative Self-Improving Python, which is a way to optimise LLM prompts. Previously, other optimisers such as COPRO and OPRO have been compared. In this blog post, I would like to deep dive into the features of MIPROv2. Nowadays, LLM\u2019s main problem is hallucination. To reduce this hallucination and evaluate the LM models, prompt engineering and optimisation methods are becoming crucial. That\u2019s why we will explore MIPROv2\u2019s features and capabilities with a use case. Before diving into DSPy, it helps to clarify what we mean by *optimisation*. In general, optimisation refers to a set of methods aimed at achieving a desired goal while maximising performance with minimal effort. The notion of \u201ceffort\u201d can vary depending on the domain \u2014 it may refer to time, computational cost, or other resources. DSPy is a framework designed for building systematic LLM pipelines.",

The instruction generation is data-aware and demonstration-aware. Uses Bayesian Optimization to effectively search over the space of generation instructions/demonstrations across your modules. ... GEPA: Uses LM's to reflect on the DSPy program's trajectory, to identify what worked, what didn't and propose prompts addressing the gaps. Additionally, GEPA can leverage domain-specific textual feedback to rapidly improve the DSPy program. Detailed tutorials on using GEPA are available at dspy.GEPA Tutorials. This optimizer is used to fine-tune the underlying LLM(s). BootstrapFinetune: Distills a prompt-based DSPy program into weight updates. Both implement MCP\u2019s JSON\u2011RPC 2.0 schema across transports, but they differ in ergonomics, transport readiness, and validation ...\n\nIf you\u2019re building tools, resources, or prompts for AI assistants via the Model Context Protocol (MCP), the SDK you choose sets your day-to-day developer experience and deployment path. In 2025, the two most common choices are the official TypeScript SDK and the Python ecosystem (notably FastMCP). Both implement MCP\u2019s JSON\u2011RPC 2.0 schema across transports, but they differ in ergonomics, transport readiness, and validation approaches. For protocol definitions and the latest transport guidance, see the 2025 spec\u2019s overview of stdio, HTTP+SSE, and Streamable HTTP in the official MCP transports documentation (2025-03-26). This review stays scenario-first and neutral: there\u2019s no universal \u201cwinner,\u201d only better fits for specific teams and constraints. Notes: Transport status reflects 2025 sources cited below; always confirm the latest repo/docs before committing to a path.\n\nExpose tools (invokable functions), resources (read-only data), and prompts (parameterized templates) Communicate via stdio for local/secure use, and via HTTP-based transports for remote use \u00b7 Serialize messages as JSON\u2011RPC 2.0 with the framing/auth rules the spec expects \u00b7 The 2025 spec positions Streamable HTTP as the modern remote transport while keeping SSE available for compatibility; the shift is recorded in the MCP spec changelog (2025-06-18). What it is: The official TypeScript SDK for building MCP servers on Node.js, with strongly-typed developer ergonomics and examples that register tools/resources/prompts. Current transport picture: stdio and HTTP+SSE are in active use in examples. As of early\u2013mid 2025, a tracked discussion indicated Streamable HTTP support was still pending; see the TypeScript SDK\u2019s issue noting Streamable HTTP status (Mar 2025).\n\nTransports and auth: For remote servers, follow the spec\u2019s session/auth guidance, enforce Origin checks, and use secure, non-deterministic session IDs. The spec consolidated these rules in mid\u20112025; see the MCP security and transport guidance (2025) for the key shifts and links to the security best practices from that release. Filesystem safety: If you expose a file server, restrict operations to an allow\u2011list of root directories, canonicalize paths to prevent traversal, and set read size limits. Validation strategy: In TypeScript, pair your types with runtime schema validators (e.g., zod) so inputs are actually checked. In Python, define Pydantic models for complex payloads to get structured errors and safer defaults. Human-in-the-loop: Modern MCP clients (e.g., VS Code) increasingly prompt for confirmation on sensitive tool calls; design your tools assuming a consent layer is present but not infallible.\n\nDeveloper ergonomics: If your team lives in VS Code and already runs Node in production, the static typing, npm workflows, and DX are hard to beat. Maintenance and maturity: Frequent 2025 updates and active examples keep the SDK current with spec changes. ... Editor-adjacent servers (docs lookup, code search, quick actions) that benefit from TypeScript\u2019s autocompletion and tight VS Code loops \u00b7 Web and API-adjacent integrations where your existing Node tooling and CI/CD already hum ... If you need Streamable HTTP right now for remote hosting, verify implementation status (or plan to operate via HTTP+SSE until Streamable lands) Runtime validation is as strong as the schemas you define\u2014TypeScript\u2019s compile-time types don\u2019t automatically enforce input constraints at runtime \u00b7 What it is: A Python-first MCP server framework with decorator-based APIs, async support, and automatic model/schema handling via type hints and Pydantic.",

Context signals: theme, displayMode, maxHeight, safeArea, view, userAgent, and locale let you adapt UI and copy.\n\nComfortable with TypeScript or Python and a web bundler (Vite, esbuild, etc.). Built UI bundle that exports a root script (React or vanilla). ... your-chatgpt-app/ \u251c\u2500 server/ \u2502 \u2514\u2500 src/index.ts # MCP server + tool handlers \u251c\u2500 web/ \u2502 \u251c\u2500 src/component.tsx # React widget \u2502 \u2514\u2500 dist/app.{js,css} # Bundled assets referenced by the server \u2514\u2500 package.json \u00b7 A user prompt causes ChatGPT to call one of your MCP tools. Your server runs the handler, fetches authoritative data, and returns structuredContent, _meta, and UI metadata. ChatGPT loads the HTML template linked in the tool descriptor (served as text/html+skybridge) and injects the payload through window.openai. The widget renders from window.openai.toolOutput, persists UI state with window.openai.setWidgetState, and can call tools again via window.openai.callTool.\n\nSet _meta[\"openai/visibility\"] on the tool descriptor to \"private\" when a tool should be callable from your widget but hidden from the model. This helps avoid awkward prompts or unsafe UX. Visibility defaults to \"public\"; private tools still work with window.openai.callTool. \"_meta\": { \"openai/outputTemplate\": \"ui://widget/kanban-board.html\", \"openai/widgetAccessible\": true, \"openai/visibility\": \"private\" } MCP tools can include tool annotations that describe the tool\u2019s potential impact. ChatGPT uses these hints to classify tools and decide when to ask the user for confirmation (elicitation) before using the tool. ... readOnlyHint: Set to true for tools that do not change state (search, lookups, previews). This won\u2019t require elicitation. openWorldHint: Set to false for tools that only affect a bounded target (for example, \u201cupdate a task by id\u201d in your own product).\n\nTypeScript SDK \u2013 Ideal when your stack is already Node/React. Repo: modelcontextprotocol/typescript-sdk, published as @modelcontextprotocol/sdk. Docs live on modelcontextprotocol.io. In other words, text/html+skybridge marks the file as a widget template instead of generic HTML. It is often described as \"the USB-C port for AI\", providing a uniform way to connect LLMs to resources they can use. It may be easier to think of it as an API, but specifically designed for LLM interactions. MCP servers can: Expose data through Resources (think of these sort of like GET endpoints; they are used to load information into the LLM's context) Provide functionality through Tools (sort of like POST endpoints; they are used to execute code or otherwise produce a side effect) Define interaction patterns through Prompts (reusable templates for LLM interactions) ... FastMCP provides a high-level, Pythonic interface for building, managing, and interacting with these servers. FastMCP handles all the complex protocol details so you can focus on building.\n\nThe central object representing your MCP application. It holds your tools, resources, and prompts, manages connections, and can be configured with settings like authentication. from fastmcp import FastMCP # Create a server instance mcp = FastMCP(name=\"MyAssistantServer\") Learn more in the FastMCP Server Documentation. Tools allow LLMs to perform actions by executing your Python functions (sync or async). Ideal for computations, API calls, or side effects (like POST/PUT). FastMCP handles schema generation from type hints and docstrings. Tools can return various types, including text, JSON-serializable objects, and even images or audio aided by the FastMCP media helper classes. @mcp.tool def multiply(a: float, b: float) -> float: \"\"\"Multiplies two numbers.\"\"\" return a * b \u00b7 Learn more in the Tools Documentation. Resources expose read-only data sources (like GET requests).\n\nThe fast, Pythonic way to build MCP servers and clients. ... FastMCP pioneered Python MCP development, and FastMCP 1.0 was incorporated into the official MCP SDK in 2024. This is FastMCP 2.0 \u2014 the actively maintained, production-ready framework that extends far beyond basic protocol implementation. While the SDK provides core functionality, FastMCP 2.0 delivers everything needed for production: advanced MCP patterns (server composition, proxying, OpenAPI/FastAPI generation, tool transformation), enterprise auth (Google, GitHub, WorkOS, Azure, Auth0, and more), deployment tools, testing utilities, and comprehensive client libraries. For production MCP applications, install FastMCP: pip install fastmcp ... FastMCP 3.0 is in development and may include some breaking changes. To avoid unexpected issues, pin your dependency to v2: fastmcp<3 \u00b7 FastMCP is the standard framework for building MCP applications, providing the fastest path from idea to production.\n\n# Static resource @mcp.resource(\"config://version\") def get_version(): return \"2.0.1\" # Dynamic resource template @mcp.resource(\"users://{user_id}/profile\") def get_profile(user_id: int): # Fetch profile for user_id... return {\"name\": f\"User {user_id}\", \"status\": \"active\"} Learn more in the Resources & Templates Documentation. New JavaScript and Web Development content every day.\n\nModal Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard for communication between AI models and external tools, data sources, and environments. Think of MCP as the bridge between AI and the outside world \u2014 it allows AI agents to go beyond static text generation and dynamically fetch data, perform actions, and integrate with applications in a safe and standardized way. Every time you integrate an AI agent with an external API (Google Drive, Slack, GitHub), you write a custom wrapper. Thousands of developers do the same thing. What if we had a universal standard for AI integrations? That\u2019s MCP (Model Context Protocol). Every AI agent framework supports tool calling \u2014 but it comes with a cost: You need to write a wrapper for every API. Every other developer does the same for the same API. If you want to reuse it in another AI system, you start over. Instead of everyone reinventing the wheel, MCP defines a universal protocol for AI integrations.\n\nprompt: crypto_market_summary \u2014 Summarize top cryptocurrencies or biggest movers \u00b7",
